## Page 1


DIFFERENTIAL OPERATORS ON LOCALLY ANALYTIC SHIMURA
VARIETIES
YUANYANG JIANG
Abstract. We investigate infinite-level Shimura varieties within the framework of analytic stacks of
Clausen-Scholze, developing their smooth, completed, locally analytic, and de Rham realizations. We
formulate a Grothendieck-Messing-Hodge-Tate period map, and establish a Grothendieck-Messing
theory for locally analytic infinite-level Shimura varieties. This theory, combined with a reformulation
of Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, implies that the locally analytic infinite-level Shimura variety
can be fully reconstructed purely from its perfectoid counterpart and its B+
dR-thickening. Building
upon this geometric structure, we systematically construct differential operators generalizing those of
Pan, and we introduce a Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex based on dual BGG complexes,
conjecturing its automorphic properties. These constructions will be used to establish a locally
analytic Jacquet-Langlands correspondence in a companion paper ([Jia26a]).

1. Introduction
This work originated from the author’s attempt to understand the work of Lue Pan ([Pan22],
[Pan26]) in a more geometric framework. In this article, we will discuss various realizations of the
infinite-level Shimura varieties in the category of analytic stacks developed by Clausen-Scholze.
In a companion paper ([Jia26a]), we apply the results here to construct a locally analytic Jacquet-
Langlands correspondence ([Jia26a, Theorem 3.3.13]). This correspondence relates cohomology of
different quaternionic Shimura varieties, and serves as a crucial ingredient for showing a classicality
theorem in the setting of Hilbert modular forms.
Given a Shimura datum (G, X) with its Hodge cocharacter µ, we have the associated tower of
Shimura varieties {XK}K⊂G(Af) over the reflex field E ⊂C. We fix a prime p and ι : C ∼= ¯Qp, and
let Ep be the closure of E ⊂ι ¯Qp. Let XK,Ep be the analytification of XK ⊗E Ep, and let XK :=
XK,Ep ⊗Ep Cp. Let FlG,µ be the partial flag variety defined over E, let FlG,µ,Ep := (FlG,µ ⊗E Ep)an,
and FlG,µ := FlG,µ,Ep ⊗Ep Cp. See §3.1.1 for details.
Perfectoid infinite-level Shimura varieties were first introduced by [Sch15], and subsequently
generalized by [CS17], [She17], [HJ23]. For any neat compact open subgroup Kp ⊂G(Ap
f), one

## Page 3


DIFFERENTIAL OPERATORS ON LOCALLY ANALYTIC SHIMURA VARIETIES
3
The aforementioned works show that X ⋄
Kp is representable by a perfectoid space XKp, when (G, X)
is of pre-abelian type ([HJ23]).
Since the test objects of the category of diamonds are perfectoid spaces, there is an implicit
p-adic completion in the limit process above. Prior to the introduction of the analytic stacks by
Clausen-Scholze, this seemed to be the only plausible construction, which is well-suited for the study
of étale or proétale cohomology.
On the other hand, after the works of Lue Pan ([Pan22], [Pan26]) and Rodríguez Camargo
([Rod22]), the subspace of G(Qp)-locally analytic vectors in the completed cohomology of Shimura
varieties ([Eme06]) has drawn considerable attention. Over this subspace with its finer topology, it
is possible to realize several p-adic Hodge theoretical constructions, such as arithmetic Sen operators
and Fontaine operators, which provides a general framework for proving classicality theorems.
Moreover, these constructions admit an enhancement on the level of sheaves on XKp. A central
role is played by Ola
Kp, the subsheaf of the structure sheaf OXKp over XKp consisting of G(Qp)-locally
analytic vectors. In [Pan26], Pan further defines several (logarithmic) differential operators on certain
subsheaves of Ola
Kp that describe the Fontaine operator.
This shows that Ola
Kp has quite interesting and non-trivial differential structures, which can not be
seen directly on the perfectoid Shimura varieties, since perfectoid spaces always have trivial sheaves
of differentials (relative to Cp). Our primary goal is to give a geometrical reformulation of these
differential operators using a more refined locally analytic version of infinite-level Shimura varieties
in the category of analytic stacks.
Remark 1.0.1. It is worth noting the recent work of Sean Howe ([How25]), which provides an
alternative framework for studying differential operators on infinite-level Shimura varieties.
1.1. Shimura varieties as analytic stacks. It is natural to consider a ringed space X la
Kp whose
underlying topological space is |XKp| and whose structure sheaf is Ola
Kp. Because it is crucial to
remember the topological structure of Ola
Kp, the theory of Clausen-Scholze analytic stacks provides
the ideal framework.
In broad terms, Clausen and Scholze define analytic rings as (animated) topological rings with
specified convergence conditions on their modules. These rings are quite general and, notably, need
not be p-adically complete. Analytic stacks are then defined as sheaves (of anima) on the site of
analytic rings equipped with the !-topology (see §2.1 for details). For an analytic ring R, we write
AnSpec(R) for the associated analytic stack via the Yoneda lemma.
As we will explain in §2.5, schemes and rigid varieties can be realized as analytic stacks. In
particular, we have XKpKp and XKpKp as analytic stacks.
We utilize the flexibility of analytic stacks to define various versions of infinite-level Shimura
varieties:
• (Smooth version, Definition 3.2.7) Define X sm
Kp := lim
←−Kp XKpKp, where the limit is taken in
the category of analytic stacks.
• (Completed version, Lemma 3.2.4) We define an analytic stack XKp over Cp. When (G, X) is
of pre-abelian type, XKp coincides with the perfectoid space representing the diamond X ⋄
Kp.
• (Locally analytic version, Definition 3.3.16) The sheaf Ola
Kp ∈Dan(XKp, Cp) naturally lifts to
a quasi-coherent sheaf Ola
Kp ∈QCoh(X sm
Kp), and we define X la
Kp := AnSpecX sm
Kp(Ola
Kp), where
AnSpec denotes the relative spectrum (see §2.3).
• (B+
dR-version, Lemma 3.9.1) We define an analytic stack B+
dR(XKp) over B+
dR, which is
a thickening of XKp. When (G, X) is of pre-abelian type, B+
dR(XKp) is given by gluing
Spf(B+
dR(A)) for open affinoid subspaces Spa(A, A+) ⊂XKp.

1.2. de Rham stacks. The constructions in §1.1 are largely formal. The first non-trivial input for
our study is the theory of de Rham stacks, which unifies various aspects of the Fontaine operators.
The concept of de Rham stacks first appeared in the work of Simpson ([Sim96]) and has seen
renewed interest following the works of Drinfeld ([Dri22], [Dri24]) and Bhatt-Lurie ([BL22b], [BL22a]).
Rodríguez Camargo ([Rod24]) generalized this construction to the setting of analytic stacks. He
introduces two variants: algebraic de Rham stacks Xalg.dR and analytic de Rham stacks XdR (see
§2.6). For a map f : X →Y , there are also relative versions Xalg.dR/Y and XdR/Y . The analytic
variant is further developed in [ABLRS25].
Remark 1.2.1. To work with toroidal compactifications of Shimura varieties, we will define in §2.7 a
logarithmic version of algebraic de Rham stacks Xalg.dR/L
log

1.3. Grothendieck-Messing-Hodge-Tate period maps. In this part, we explain a Grothendieck-
Messing theory for locally analytic Shimura varieties, showing that its infinitesimal structure is
equivalent to what we term the “Grothendieck-Messing-Hodge-Tate period domain”. This aligns with
the expectation that p-adic Shimura varieties parametrize variations of p-adic Hodge structures.
The classical Grothendieck-Messing theory and Serre-Tate theory show that the deformation
theory of abelian varieties is controlled by flag varieties. For Shimura varieties, let Flstd
G,µ be the

1.4. Differential operators. We use Theorem 1.3.3 to construct and generalize the differential
operators dk and ¯dk in [Pan26].

1.5. Organization of the paper. The paper consists of two parts. Section 2 provides the general
prerequisites including general discussions about the theory of Clausen-Scholze analytic stacks and
its solid and Tate variants ([Rod24]), the construction of a logarithmic version of algebraic de Rham
stacks, and the study of filtered BGG complexes on partial flag varieties. Section 3 applies the
general results to the setting of Shimura varieties. We refer the readers to the introduction to each
section for more details.

1.7. Notation. We will work with solid modules in the sense of [CS19]. To ease the notation, all
the tensor products will be taken in the solid sense, which we will simply denote as −ˆ⊗−.
We fix ¯Q ⊂C, and we fix an isomorphism ι : C ∼= ¯Qp. Denote by Cp the completion of ¯Qp for the p-
adic topology. By Frobenius, we will always refer to the geometric Frobenius. Let χcycl : GalQ →Z×
p
denote the cyclotomic character, sending Frl to l−1. By our convention, χcycl has Hodge-Tate weight
−1. Our convention for local class field theory takes the uniformizer to a lift of the geometric
Frobenius.
2. Preliminary on analytic stacks
This section collects some technical results on solid and Tate stacks, algebraic and analytic de
Rham stacks, and a log version of algebraic de Rham stacks. The main references are [Rod24] and
[ABLRS25]. Many results presented here are due to Clausen-Scholze.
The section is structured as follows:
Subsection 2.1 introduces the general definition of analytic stacks of Clausen-Scholze and
[ABLRS25]. We also discuss general six functor formalisms, following [Man22a], [Man22b], [Sch26],
[HM24], and [Rod24].
Subsection 2.2 introduces the solid and Tate stacks of [Rod24]. Since we need to work with
both types of stacks, we also study the relation between the two categories, and introduce so-called
“essentially Tate” stacks, for which the functor into Tate stacks is fully faithful.
In Subsection 2.3, we define so-called “affine proper morphisms", and provide a solid stack version
of the relative Spec construction. Many spaces that later arise will come from this construction.
In Subsection 2.4, we recall the Rees construction realizing filtered objects as quasi-coherent
sheaves on [A1/Gm] as in [Mou21], [BL22a]. We further define a (relative) Spf construction associate
a solid stack over [A1/Gm] to a filtered complete algebra.
In Subsection 2.5, we give some examples of solid stacks, notably various geometric realizations of
p-adic Lie groups and vector bundles.
In Subsection 2.6, we give the definition of the algebraic and analytic de Rham stacks of [Rod24],
and prove some basic properties. Many of the results in this subsection are essentially proven in
[ABLRS25], except that we work with Tate stacks where (compared with Gelfand stacks) all the
test objects are not equipped with induced analytic structures. We do not claim any originality over
these results.
In Subsection 2.7 we give a log version of filtered algebraic de Rham stack. Given a log smooth
rigid variety X over L, Xalg.dR/L,+
log
is a solid stack over [A1/Gm]. We also prove that the category
of quasi-coherent sheaves on Xalg.dR/L,+
log
is equivalent to that of filtered DX/L,log-modules. We also


## Page 10


10
YUANYANG JIANG
show that the filtered complete version Xalg.dR/L,ˆ+
log
is affine when X = Spa(A, A+), in the sense
that the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on Xalg.dR/L,ˆ+
log
is equivalent to that of filtered complete
dRlog(A)-modules. The two interpretations have their own benefits.
In Subsection 2.8, we compare the relation between analytic and algebraic de Rham stacks. The
main new result is a proof of an identification of the categories of vector bundles on analytic and
algebraic de Rham stacks.
In Subsection 2.9, we prove some results in representation theory which will be used later. In
§2.9.1, we discuss the de Rham stacks of partial flag varieties. One key construction is FlP / \
(g0/n0)
as in [Sch24], which should relate to twisted D-modules via Beilinson-Bernstein localization. In
§2.9.2, we show how to realize the representations of Lie algebras on a suitable classifying stack,
following [Sch24]. We also construct the infinitesimal action using geometry. In §2.9.3, we define the
parabolic BGG category as a subcategory of the category of equivariant D-modules on partial flag
varieties, and we compare this definition to more classical definitions. Finally, in §2.9.4, we construct
the (dual) BGG complexes on partial flag varieties following [Fal83].
In Subsection 2.10, we realize the de Rham period sheaf of [Sch13] and [DLLZ22] as a quasicoherent
sheaf on the filtered algebraic log de Rham stacks. We actually give two realizations: one depends
on the period sheaf OBdR,log, while the other is by deformation. The key observation is that the two
coincide. Later in Subsections 3.9 and 3.10, we will reformulation the logarithmic Riemann-Hilbert
correspondence over Shimura varieties over the filtered algebraic log de Rham stacks.
2.1. Analytic stacks. The main reference is the YouTube video of the course given by Clausen-
Scholze, [Rod24] and [ABLRS25].
Later, we will work mainly with the solid and Tate stacks
introduced in [Rod24] which we will introduce in §2.2.
Definition 2.1.1 (Clausen-Scholze). A pre-analytic ring is a pair A := (A▷, D≥0(A)) where A▷
is an animated condensed ring, and D≥0(A) is a full subcategory of D≥0(A▷) such that after
stabilization, D(A) is a full stable subcategory closed under all limits, colimits, and internal Hom’s,
and D≥0(A) = D≥0(A▷) ∩D(A).
We say A is an analytic ring if the unit A▷∈D≥0(A▷) lies in the subcategory D≥0(A).
We denote by (−)∧
A : D(A▷) →D(A) the left adjunction to the inclusion functor. Note that D(A)
has a symmetric monoidal structure.
Definition 2.1.2. A map between analytic rings A →B is a map f : A▷→B▷with the condition
that the forgetful functor D(B▷) →D(A▷) takes the full subcategory D(B) to D(A).
In this case, we denote by f∗: D(B) →D(A) the forgetful functor, and by f∗its left adjoint, that
is, the pull-back functor M 7→(B▷⊗A M)∧
B.
Definition 2.1.3. We say f : A →B is proper if B has the induced analytic structure, in the
sense that D≥0(B) ⊂ModB▷(D≥0(A)). In other word, M ∈D≥0(B▷) lies in D≥0(B) if and only if
M|A▷∈D≥0(A). In this case, we write B := (B▷, A), which we refer to as the analytic structure
induced from A.
Given any f : A →B, there is a unique decomposition

We say f : A →B is an open immersion if f∗: D(A) →D(B) admits a left adjoint that is fully
faithful, which we denote as
f! : D(B) →D(A).

Theorem 2.1.7. Let f : X →Y in AnStk(C ).
(1) If f satisfies universal !-descent, then f satisfies universal ∗-descent.
(2) If f is a suave !-cover, then f satisfies universal !-descent.
(3) If f is a prim !-cover, then f satisfies universal !-descent.
(4) Consider the pull-back diagram (3). Let A ∈QCoh(X). If g satisfies universal ∗-descent, and
g′,∗(A) is f′-suave (resp. f-prim), then A is f-suave (resp. f-prim).
(5) Consider g : Z →X. If g is suave (resp. prim), then A ∈QCoh(X) is f-suave (resp. f-prim)
implies that g∗(A) is (f ◦g)-suave (resp. f ◦g-prim). The inverse is true if g is a suave (resp. prim)
!-cover. In that case, Df◦g(g∗(A)) ∼= g∗Df(A) ⊗Dg(OZ) (resp. Pf◦g(g∗(A)) ∼= g∗Pf(A) ⊗Pg(OZ)).
In particular, the class of cohomological smooth (resp. co-smooth) maps is closed under composi-
tions.
(6) If f is a !-surjection, then f satisfies universal ∗-descent.
(7) If f satisfies suave !-descent, and X and Y are ω1-compact, then f is a !-surjection.
Proof. (1)-(3) are the contents of [Sch26, Theorem 5.12 & Propositions 6.19 & 6.20] respectively.
(4) is given by [Man22b, Corollary 7.8] and [Rod24, Proposition 3.1.4]. (5) is given by [Man22b,
Corollary 8.6] and [Rod24, Proposition 3.1.24]. (6) follows from [ABLRS25, Remark 4.2.3] since
“ lim
−→[n]∈∆op ”Xn/Y →Y is a !-equivalence. Note that to prove !-ability, in (4) we use “local on the
target” and in (5) we use “local on the source” of [Sch26, Theorem 5.19].
For (7), consider the Čech nerve {Xn/Y }n∈∆op associated to f and let Y ′ := lim
−→n∈∆op Xn/Y . Then
by (4) and (6), X →Y ′ also satisfies suave !-descent. In particular, the monomorphism i : Y ′ →Y
satisfies suave !-descent by (5). This implies that Y ′ →Y is a !-equivalence. Since they are both
ω1-compact, Y ′ ∼= Y in AnStk(C ).
□

Definition 2.1.13. An analytic ring A = (A▷, D≥0(A)) is Fredholm if all perfect complexes over A
(i.e. dualizable objects in D(A)) arises from (discrete) perfect complexes over A▷(∗).

Fredholm.

2.2. Solid and Tate stacks. We will mainly work over the following classes of analytic rings.
Restricting to these classes allows us to define algebraic and analytic de Rham stacks as in [Rod24].
Definition 2.2.1 ([Rod24, Definition 2.6.1, 2.6.6, 2.6.10]). —
(1) For any analytic ring A over Z□, define A+ := Map(Z[T]□, A), where Z[T]□is the analytic
ring defined in [CS19, Example 7.3], and the Map is the internal Hom space of analytic rings, which
has an inherited animated ring structure.
(2) An analytic ring A = (A▷, D≥0(A)) over Z□is solid if natural map (A▷, π0(A+))□→A
is an equivalence of analytic rings. In other words, ∀M ∈D≥0(A▷), M ∈D≥0(A) if and only if
∀f ∈π0(A+)(∗) inducing Z[T] →A▷, M|Z[T] ∈D≥0(Z[T]□).
(3) An animated condensed ring A▷over Qp is bounded if A▷∼= A▷,◦[1/p] with A▷,◦(S) :=
Map(Zp⟨N[S]⟩□, A) for S extremely disconnected.
(4) An analytic ring A = (A▷, D≥0(A)) over (Qp, Zp)□is bounded if A is solid and A▷is bounded.
(5) We fix an uncountable cutoff cardinal κ ([Man22a, Definition 2.9.11]). A solid analytic ring A
is κ-small if the underlying condensed ring A▷is κ-small.
Definition 2.2.2 (Solid stacks, [Rod24, Definition 3.2.7]). Let C := AnRingZ□,κ be the category of
κ-small solid analytic rings over Z□. A (κ-small) solid stack is defined to be an analytic stack over
AnRingZ□,κ (Definition 2.1.5), and we denote SolidStkκ := AnStk(AnRingZ□,κ). We will omit the
subscript κ from now on.

2.3. Affine proper morphisms. For our later purpose, we need the following class of morphisms:
Definition 2.3.1. Let f : X →Y be a morphism between solid stacks. We say f is affine proper if
there exists a !-surjection Y ′ = `
i∈I AnSpec(Ai) →Y , such that X ×Y AnSpec(Ai) ∼= AnSpec(Bi),
where Ai →Bi are proper morphisms between analytic rings.
We say that f is affine proper and descendable (of index ≤n) if in addition, one can take Y ′ →Y
to be an open covering, and for each i, Ai →Bi is descendable (of index ≤n) in the sense of [Mat16].
For later purpose, we introduce the following notion:
Definition 2.3.2. Let f : X →Y be a morphism between solid stacks. We say that f is essentially
affine proper and descendable (of index ≤n), if there exists g : X′ →X such that f ◦g : X′ →Y is
affine proper, and descendable (of index ≤n).
Proposition 2.3.3 (Clausen-Scholze). If f is essentially affine proper and descendable, then f is a
!-surjection and is cohomologically co-smooth.
Proof. By definition, we are reduced to the case of a proper and descendable morphism f : X =
AnSpec(B) →Y = AnSpec(A). It is a !-cover directly by the descendability assumption. Then we
conclude by Theorem 2.1.7 (3). The cohomologically co-smooth is given by Theorem 2.1.7 (4) and
(6).
□

2.7. Logarithmic de Rham stack. In this subsection, we introduce a version of the “algebraic
logarithmic de Rham stack" (Definition 2.7.6).
Before we start, let us explain the idea of our construction: for smooth rigid varieties X =
Spa(A, A+) over L, by Theorem 2.6.5 (1)(3), the algebraic de Rham stack (relative to L) is isomorphic
to X/ ˆ∆X, where ˆ∆X is the completion along the Zariski closed immersion ∆: X ,→X ×LX regarded
as an ind-rigid varieties. Therefore, to define a log version of the algebraic de Rham stack, we want
a log version of ˆ∆X.
One way of reconstructing ˆ∆X is to consider the fiber product X ×Xalg.dR X. On the other hand,
one expects (the filtered complete version of) Xalg.dR to be affine, and isomorphic to Spa(dR(A)),
for example in the sense of [AM25] or [BL22b, Definition 7.15] (see Lemma 2.7.18 below). Therefore,


## Page 33


DIFFERENTIAL OPERATORS ON LOCALLY ANALYTIC SHIMURA VARIETIES
33
one may expect that ˆ∆X ∼= Spf(A ⊗dR(A) A) in a suitable sense. This turns out to be true (see
Lemma 2.7.3 (2)). This also enables us to define a log version of ˆ∆X, by simply replacing dR(A) by
dRlog(A).
Remark 2.7.1. When the log structure is induced by a strict normal crossings divisor, the log de
Rham stack has been constructed in [Bar25], following an idea of Martin Olsson. In general, one
expects to define it as the relative de Rham stack with respect to the stack of log structures, where
the latter has been studied in [Zha26].

2.8. Analytic versus algebraic de Rham stacks. We also need the following relation between
analytic and algebraic de Rham stacks. The new result is the identification of the categories of vector
bundles. Recall that Xalg.dR/L is essentially Tate by Lemma 2.7.12, and Xalg.dR/L := (Xalg.dR/L)Tate.

is symmetric monoidal.

3. Shimura varieties as analytic stacks
In this section, we work with general Shimura varieties. We want to study the locally analytic
vectors in the structure sheaf of infinite-level Shimura varieties, following [Pan22] and [Rod22]. In
the framework of analytic stacks, we will define so-called locally analytic Shimura varieties. The two
main results are Theorem 3.4.6 and Theorem 3.10.1.
Theorem 3.4.6 says roughly that the geometry of locally analytic Shimura varieties is determined
by its de Rham stack plus a so-called “Grothendieck-Messing-Hodge-Tate period map”. We learn
about this period map first in the local setting in [DR24], but the version for global Shimura varieties
is more subtle, for which we first need to develop a theory of “Grothendieck-Messing period map” in
Subsection 3.1.


## Page 60


60
YUANYANG JIANG
Theorem 3.10.1 is about the analytic de Rham stack of perfectoid spaces. We explain how one
can fit the (logarithmic) Riemann-Hilbert correspondence into our framework.
Here is a more detailed plan:
In Subsection 3.1, we give a reformulation of the classical Kodaira-Spencer isomorphism (Proposi-
tions 3.1.8 & 3.1.12), which could be understood as a characteristic 0 version of Grothendieck-Messing
theory, but note that our results hold for any Shimura varieties. As a corollary, we give a reformulation
of (dual) BGG complexes on Shimura varieties.
In Subsection 3.2, we define two versions—a completed version (Lemma 3.2.4) and a smooth
/ uncompleted version (Definition 3.2.7)—of the original construction of the “perfectoid Shimura
variety" of [Sch15], in the frame work of analytic stacks. The slight trickiness comes from the toroidal
boundaries. The completed infinite-level Shimura varieties also admit Hodge-Tate period maps to
FlG,µ in the category of Tate stacks.
In Subsection 3.3, we introduce the derived locally analytic vectors as defined in [RR22], [RR23],
and apply the construction to define the main object of our study—locally analytic Shimura varieties.
We show that its cohomology calculates the locally analytic part of the completed cohomology of
[Eme06].
In Subsection 3.4, we define a so-called “Grothendieck-Messing-Hodge-Tate period map”, which
combines the Hodge-Tate period map and the Grothendieck-Messing period map. We state a theorem
that could be understood as a Grothendieck-Messing theory of locally analytic Shimura varieties
(Theorem 3.4.6). This is one of the deepest results in the section.
In Subsection 3.5, we recall the geometric Sen theory from [Pan22], [Pil24], [Rod22], which is a
key tool for studying locally analytic Shimura varieties. This will allow us to relate locally analytic
Shimura varieties to smooth infinite-level Shimura varieties in Subsection 3.6 (Proposition 3.6.1).
In Subsection 3.7, we further relate smooth infinite-level Shimura varieties to its de Rham stack.
Since we don’t know whether taking algebraic de Rham stacks commutes with limits in general, the
latter is defined as the inverse limit of de Rham stacks of finite level Shimura varieties. Combining
Subsections 3.6 & 3.7, we complete the proof of Theorem 3.4.6.
In Subsection 3.8, we study the “horizontal action” of m0, and define the horizontal locally analytic
Shimura variety, which is the quotient of the locally analytic Shimura variety by this action. The
key result is Corollary 3.8.9, which follows immediately from Theorem 3.4.6. shows in particular
that the map from the horizontal locally analytic Shimura variety X la,0
Kp to XKpKp × FlG,µ is “étale”
in a suitable sense. In the next section, we will use this to pull back the classical theta operators on
XKpKp and FlG,µ to define the operators dkτ
τ
and ¯dkτ
τ .
Subsections 3.9 and 3.10 focus on another topic: a reformulation of the logarithmic Riemann-
Hilbert correspondence on the Shimura varieties.
In Subsection 3.9, we start with defining a
B+
dR-version of the infinite-level Shimura varieties. This is essentially in Subsection 2.10, except
some additional treatment is needed when the perfectoidness of the infinite-level Shimura varieties
is unknown. In Subsection 3.10, we reformulate the logarithmic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence
(Theorem 3.10.1, Corollary 3.10.7). The key point is that the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence can
be formulated in a way that is intrinsic to a perfectoid space equipped with an action of a p-adic Lie
group.
In Subsection 3.11, we give an account of the previous results in the setting of basic local Shimura
varieties ([SW20]). Part of these results are essentially in [DR24].
3.1. Grothendieck-Messing theory.

3.2. Infinite-level Shimura varieties. The goal of this subsection is to introduce two versions of
the infinite-level Shimura varieties—the completed version X tor
Kp (Lemma 3.2.4) and the uncompleted /
smooth version X tor,sm
Kp
(Definition 3.2.7), and we will show that the natural map from the completed
version to the smooth version is a !-covering.
The construction of the infinite-level Shimura varieties was introduced by [Sch15] and further
studied in [CS17], [She17], [HJ23]. Away from the boundary, one defines XKp := lim
←−KpKp XKpKp,
as a diamond, which is represented by a perfectoid space by [HJ23] when the Shimura datum is of
pre-abelian type. However, we do not know a general functor from the category of diamonds to the
category of Tate stacks, sending perfectoid spaces to the associated Tate stack, as proétale covers
are not necessarily !-covers. Therefore, some non-formal argument is needed for the realization of
XKp as a Tate stack.
We continue with the setting of Notation 3.1.1. In particular, we fix a base level Kp,0, and a cone
decomposition, such that the associated toroidal compactification X tor
KpKp,0 for XKpKp,0 is smooth
whose boundary is a normal crossings divisor. For each Kp, by rigid Abhyankar’s lemma ([DLLZ22,

Definition 3.5.6 ([Rod26, Definition 2.3.4]). Let U be a quasi-compact open subspace of Fl, and
let Kp be an open compact subgroup of Gc(Zp) that acts on U. We say that F ∈QCohgc(U) is a
relative Kp-analytic modules, if F is associated to a direct summand of an ON Banach O(U)-module
V , that admits an O+(U)-lattice V 0 with a basis {vi}i∈I, such that there exist ϵ > 0, such that Kp
acts on vi mod pϵ trivially.

We say that F is a relatively locally analytic module if there exists a filtered colimit F ∼= lim
−→i∈I Fi
in QCohgc(U), such that ∀i ∈I, there exists an open covering U = ∪j∈JiUi,j, such that for ∀j ∈Ji,
Fi|Ui,j ∈QCohgc(Ui,j) is relatively Ki,j-analytic for some open compact subgroup Ki,j.
If F is relatively locally analytic, the action of Kp on RΓ(U, F) is locally analytic, and we have
an action of gc (and thus of gc,0) on F. If n0 acts on F by zero, then F gives rise to an object in
QCohgc(U)n0.
Notation 3.5.7. We denote as QCohrla
gc (U)n0 the subcategory of QCohgc(U)n0 generated (under
filtered colimits, extensions, and taking idempotents) by relative locally analytic modules that are
killed by n0.
Note that the underlying sheaves of relative locally analytic modules are colimits of ON Banach
modules by definition, and QCohrla
gc (U)n0 is not a stable ∞-category.

Remark 3.8.19. The parameter α should determine the automorphic property, and different choices
of β are related by translation functors.
Remark 3.8.20. When the Levi Mµ is abelian, one can show that the complex BGGFα,β is indeed
induced by the Fontaine operators on the Galois side as in [Pan26]. However, in the general case,
the precise relation is not clear to the author.
We believe that answering the following questions should be useful for proving classicality and for
understanding the conjectural locally analytic p-adic Langlands correspondence.

Remark 3.8.22. In the case of modular curves, the part (1) was affirmed by [Pan26]. Subsequent
generalizations of Pan’s work ([QS25], [Mat25]) extended the results to the cases for quaternionic
Shimura curves and for Shimura surfaces associated to rank 2 unramified unitary groups.
The parts (3) and (4) were affirmed in the case of modular curves in [Pan26] away from the
Steinberg case. [QS25] extended these results to the setting of quaternionic Shimura curves, including
the Steinberg case.
In a companion paper ([Jia26a]), we will give an affirmative answer to Question 3.8.21 (1) in the
setting of Hilbert modular varieties using a locally analytic Jacquet-Langlands correspondence.

3.11. Local Analogue. The results in this section have local analogue, i.e. for local Shimura
varieties. The set-up is as follows:
